This foundation has opened promising avenues for multiple therapies, including cancer treatment, particularly for aggressive malignancies like lung cancer [2], [3], [4], [5]. mRNA can be engineered to express therapeutic proteins directly at the tumor site that inhibit critical oncogenic signaling pathways, enhance immune recognition of malignant cells, or mitigate cancer-associated syndromes such as cachexia [6], [7], [8]. However, a significant hurdle persists in the clinical translation of these therapies, as currently available delivery systems fail to achieve preferential mRNA accumulation in cancer tumors after systemic administration [9], [10]."},{"#name":"para","$":{"id":"p0035","view":"all"},"_":"Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) represent a promising platform for mRNA delivery in cancer therapy [11], [12]. However, LNP formulations comprising clinically approved ionizable lipids predominantly exhibit liver tropism, limiting their utility for extra-hepatic malignancies such as lung cancer [13]. While surface functionalization with targeting ligands has been explored to redirect LNPs to tissues other than the liver [14], [15], [16], [17], [18], recently developed selective organ targeting (SORT) technology offers an alternative approach by incorporating additional specialized molecules into conventional LNP formulations [19], [20], [21], [22], [23], [24]. SORT LNPs achieve tissue specificity through protein corona formation in the bloodstream, where the chemical structure and charge properties of SORT molecules determine which plasma proteins adsorb onto the LNP surface [20], [25]. This creates an endogenous targeting system that leverages natural protein-receptor affinities to direct LNPs to specific organs without external targeting ligands [26], [27], [28], [29], [30]. However, the potential of endogenous targeting mechanisms to facilitate differential biodistribution of mRNA-loaded LNPs, not merely to specific organs but with preferential accumulation in malignant lesions within those tissues, remains largely unexplored."},{"#name":"para","$":{"id":"p0040","view":"all"},"$$":[{"#name":"__text__","_":"Vitronectin (Vtn), a plasma glycoprotein, is a critical component of the protein corona that mediates the binding of SORT LNPs to α"},{"#name":"inf","$":{"loc":"post"},"_":"v"},{"#name":"__text__","_":"β"},{"#name":"inf","$":{"loc":"post"},"_":"3"},{"#name":"__text__","_":" and α"},{"#name":"inf","$":{"loc":"post"},"_":"v"},{"#name":"__text__","_":"β"},{"#name":"inf","$":{"loc":"post"},"_":"5"},{"#name":"__text__","_":" integrin receptors expressed in lung tissue [20], [31]. Notably, these integrins are upregulated during malignant transformation, including in lung cancer cells, where they play pivotal roles in tumor angiogenesis, metastatic progression, and extracellular matrix remodeling [32], [33], [34]. This differential expression profile between malignant and healthy lung cells creates a strategic dual-targeting opportunity: Vtn-enriched protein coronas can facilitate initial pulmonary tropism of systemically administered LNPs, while subsequently enabling preferential accumulation within neoplastic lesions through enhanced integrin-mediated endocytosis, thereby improving therapeutic specificity and potentially reducing off-target effects. To exploit this targeting mechanism, we engineered conventional LNPs with optimized physicochemical properties that efficiently bind and retain plasma Vtn in their protein corona, enabling targeted delivery to lung tumors while maintaining formulation simplicity by eliminating the need for additional SORT molecules or substantial chemical modifications to the ionizable lipid component. The desired tumor-targeting formulation was achieved through a strategic substitution of cholesterol with DC-cholesterol in LNPs containing the commercially available 113-O12B ionizable lipid (DC_113) [35]. Our findings demonstrated that the distinctive interplay between the tertiary amine-containing DC-cholesterol and 113-O12B ionizable lipid in the LNP structure promotes robust binding with plasma Vtn, resulting in selective mRNA translation in the lungs of healthy mice and preferential protein expression in cancer tumors compared to surrounding lung tissue in an orthotopic cancer model."}]},{"#name":"para","$":{"id":"p0045","view":"all"},"$$":[{"#name":"__text__","_":"We evaluated the efficiency and therapeutic potential of our cancer-targeted DC_113 LNP by implementing Follistatin (FST) therapy for lung cancer and its associated cachexia. FST functions as a potent endogenous antagonist of Activin A (ActA) [36], [37], a TGF-β (Transforming Growth Factor-beta) superfamily member secreted by lung cancer tumors [38], [39], [40]. Elevated ActA levels correlate with aggressive tumor behavior, increased metastatic potential, and diminished patient survival [40], [41], [42]. The pathological effects of ActA are multifaceted, promoting cancer cell proliferation, enhancing migratory and invasive capabilities, and facilitating extracellular matrix remodeling that enables tumor dissemination [43], [44], [45]. Beyond its direct tumor-promoting effects, ActA serves as a key mediator of cancer-associated cachexia [46], a devastating wasting syndrome characterized by involuntary weight loss and muscle catabolism that affects up to 64% of patients with advanced malignancies [47]. In our experimental model, systemic delivery of "},{"#name":"italic","_":"Fst"},{"#name":"__text__","_":" mRNA via DC_113 LNPs to mice bearing orthotopic lung cancer xenografts resulted in a significant reduction of tumor burden while effectively alleviating cachexia symptoms. Collectively, these findings validate our developed DC_113 for lung cancer-targeted mRNA delivery through the exploitation of in situ endogenous protein interactions and highlight FST as a promising therapeutic strategy capable of simultaneously addressing both the primary tumor and secondary systemic manifestations of lung cancer."}]}]}]},{"#name":"section","$$":[{"#name":"section","$":{"xmlns:ce":true,"xmlns:mml":true,"xmlns:xs":true,"xmlns:xlink":true,"xmlns:xocs":true,"xmlns:tb":true,"xmlns:xsi":true,"xmlns:cals":true,"xmlns:sb":true,"xmlns:sa":true,"xmlns:ja":true,"xmlns":true,"id":"s0010","view":"all"},"$$":[{"#name":"label","_":"2"},{"#name":"section-title","$":{"id":"st0035"},"_":"Materials class="ReferencedArticles"></section><section class="ReferencedArticles"></section><div id="preview-section-abstract"><div class="PageDivider"></div><div class="Abstracts u-font-serif" id="abstracts"><div class="abstract author-highlights" id="ab0015" lang="en"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">Highlights</h2><div id="as0015"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="sp0065"><ul class="list"><li class="react-xocs-list-item"><span class="list-label">•</span><span class="list-content"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0005">Ligand-free LNPs binding serum vitronectin enable preferential mRNA delivery to integrin-expressing lung tumors.</div></span></li><li class="react-xocs-list-item"><span class="list-label">•</span><span class="list-content"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0010">DC-cholesterol–triamine lipid synergy drives vitronectin-mediated lung tropism of LNPs.</div></span></li><li class="react-xocs-list-item"><span class="list-label">•</span><span class="list-content"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0015">Apparent pK<sub>a</sub> ≥ 7.9 predicts efficient vitronectin binding and lung specificity of LNPs.</div></span></li><li class="react-xocs-list-item"><span class="list-label">•</span><span class="list-content"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0020">Tumor-targeted follistatin mRNA therapy outperforms systemic expression in treating lung cancer and cachexia.</div></span></li></ul></div></div></div><div class="abstract author" id="ab0005" lang="en"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">Abstract</h2><div id="as0005"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="sp0055">Systemic delivery of mRNA therapeutics to lung cancer tumors remains challenging despite recent advances in organ-targeting lipid nanoparticles (LNPs). This study introduces novel LNPs, comprising commercially available DC-cholesterol (a cationic derivative of cholesterol) and 113-O12B ionizable lipid (DC_113), which provide selective mRNA translation in healthy lungs while achieving preferential protein expression in pulmonary tumors compared to adjacent lung tissue in an orthotopic cancer model. Analysis of LNPs with diverse ionizable lipids revealed that DC_113 has an apparent pK<sub>a</sub> above 7.9, the threshold required for efficient interaction with plasma vitronectin (Vtn) and subsequent targeting of Vtn receptors expressed in lung tissue and upregulated in tumors. DC_113 loaded with follistatin (FST)-coded mRNA preferentially delivered therapeutic cargo to tumors, resulting in significant FST expression and subsequent inhibition of Activin A, a key driver of cancer progression and cachexia. This cancer-preferential mRNA delivery achieved approximately 2.5-fold greater reduction in tumor burden than liver-tropic LNPs producing systemic FST. Furthermore, it efficiently mitigated cancer cachexia by improving food intake, maintaining body weight, and preserving muscle and adipose tissues without adverse effects. These findings present a novel strategy for endogenous targeting of LNPs to lung tumors that can enhance the potential of mRNA-based interventions for pulmonary cancer.</div></div></div><div class="abstract graphical" id="ab0010" lang="en"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">Graphical abstract</h2><div id="as0010"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="sp0060"><span class="display"><figure class="figure text-xs" id="f0050"><span><img src="https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S0168365926002725-ga1.jpg" height="244" alt="Unlabelled Image"/><ol class="u-margin-s-bottom"><li><a class="anchor download-link u-font-sans anchor-primary" href="https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S0168365926002725-ga1_lrg.jpg" target="_blank" download="" title="Download high-res image (147KB)"><span class="anchor-text-container"><span class="anchor-text">Download: <span class="download-link-title">Download high-res image (147KB)</span></span></span></a></li><li><a class="anchor download-link u-font-sans anchor-primary" href="https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S0168365926002725-ga1.jpg" target="_blank" download="" title="Download full-size image"><span class="anchor-text-container"><span class="anchor-text">Download: <span class="download-link-title">Download full-size image</span></span></span></a></li></ol></span></figure></span></div></div></div></div></div><div id="preview-section-introduction"><div class="PageDivider"></div><div class="Introduction u-font-serif u-margin-l-ver"><h2 class="u-h4 u-margin-s-bottom">Introduction</h2><section id="s0005"><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0030">The clinical potential of messenger RNA (mRNA) technology has been demonstrated through the successful development of SARS-CoV-2 vaccines [1]. This foundation has opened promising avenues for multiple therapies, including cancer treatment, particularly for aggressive malignancies like lung cancer [2], [3], [4], [5]. mRNA can be engineered to express therapeutic proteins directly at the tumor site that inhibit critical oncogenic signaling pathways, enhance immune recognition of malignant cells, or mitigate cancer-associated syndromes such as cachexia [6], [7], [8]. However, a significant hurdle persists in the clinical translation of these therapies, as currently available delivery systems fail to achieve preferential mRNA accumulation in cancer tumors after systemic administration [9], [10].</div><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0035">Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) represent a promising platform for mRNA delivery in cancer therapy [11], [12]. However, LNP formulations comprising clinically approved ionizable lipids predominantly exhibit liver tropism, limiting their utility for extra-hepatic malignancies such as lung cancer [13]. While surface functionalization with targeting ligands has been explored to redirect LNPs to tissues other than the liver [14], [15], [16], [17], [18], recently developed selective organ targeting (SORT) technology offers an alternative approach by incorporating additional specialized molecules into conventional LNP formulations [19], [20], [21], [22], [23], [24]. SORT LNPs achieve tissue specificity through protein corona formation in the bloodstream, where the chemical structure and charge properties of SORT molecules determine which plasma proteins adsorb onto the LNP surface [20], [25]. This creates an endogenous targeting system that leverages natural protein-receptor affinities to direct LNPs to specific organs without external targeting ligands [26], [27], [28], [29], [30]. However, the potential of endogenous targeting mechanisms to facilitate differential biodistribution of mRNA-loaded LNPs, not merely to specific organs but with preferential accumulation in malignant lesions within those tissues, remains largely unexplored.</div><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0040">Vitronectin (Vtn), a plasma glycoprotein, is a critical component of the protein corona that mediates the binding of SORT LNPs to α<sub>v</sub>β<sub>3</sub> and α<sub>v</sub>β<sub>5</sub> integrin receptors expressed in lung tissue [20], [31]. Notably, these integrins are upregulated during malignant transformation, including in lung cancer cells, where they play pivotal roles in tumor angiogenesis, metastatic progression, and extracellular matrix remodeling [32], [33], [34]. This differential expression profile between malignant and healthy lung cells creates a strategic dual-targeting opportunity: Vtn-enriched protein coronas can facilitate initial pulmonary tropism of systemically administered LNPs, while subsequently enabling preferential accumulation within neoplastic lesions through enhanced integrin-mediated endocytosis, thereby improving therapeutic specificity and potentially reducing off-target effects. To exploit this targeting mechanism, we engineered conventional LNPs with optimized physicochemical properties that efficiently bind and retain plasma Vtn in their protein corona, enabling targeted delivery to lung tumors while maintaining formulation simplicity by eliminating the need for additional SORT molecules or substantial chemical modifications to the ionizable lipid component. The desired tumor-targeting formulation was achieved through a strategic substitution of cholesterol with DC-cholesterol in LNPs containing the commercially available 113-O12B ionizable lipid (DC_113) [35]. Our findings demonstrated that the distinctive interplay between the tertiary amine-containing DC-cholesterol and 113-O12B ionizable lipid in the LNP structure promotes robust binding with plasma Vtn, resulting in selective mRNA translation in the lungs of healthy mice and preferential protein expression in cancer tumors compared to surrounding lung tissue in an orthotopic cancer model.</div><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0045">We evaluated the efficiency and therapeutic potential of our cancer-targeted DC_113 LNP by implementing Follistatin (FST) therapy for lung cancer and its associated cachexia. FST functions as a potent endogenous antagonist of Activin A (ActA) [36], [37], a TGF-β (Transforming Growth Factor-beta) superfamily member secreted by lung cancer tumors [38], [39], [40]. Elevated ActA levels correlate with aggressive tumor behavior, increased metastatic potential, and diminished patient survival [40], [41], [42]. The pathological effects of ActA are multifaceted, promoting cancer cell proliferation, enhancing migratory and invasive capabilities, and facilitating extracellular matrix remodeling that enables tumor dissemination [43], [44], [45]. Beyond its direct tumor-promoting effects, ActA serves as a key mediator of cancer-associated cachexia [46], a devastating wasting syndrome characterized by involuntary weight loss and muscle catabolism that affects up to 64% of patients with advanced malignancies [47]. In our experimental model, systemic delivery of <em>Fst</em> mRNA via DC_113 LNPs to mice bearing orthotopic lung cancer xenografts resulted in a significant reduction of tumor burden while effectively alleviating cachexia symptoms. Collectively, these findings validate our developed DC_113 for lung cancer-targeted mRNA delivery through the exploitation of in situ endogenous protein interactions and highlight FST as a promising therapeutic strategy capable of simultaneously addressing both the primary tumor and secondary systemic manifestations of lung cancer.</div></section></div></div><div class="PageDivider"></div><div snippets</h2><section><section id="s0010"><section id="s0015"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">Materials</h2><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0050">113-O12B (tetrakis(2-(octyldisulfaneyl)ethyl) 3,3′,3″,3″‘-(((methylazanediyl)bis(ethane-2,1-diyl))bis(azanetriyl))tetrapropionate), 306-O12B (tetrakis(2-(octyldisulfaneyl)ethyl) class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">DC-cholesterol enables lung-specific mRNA delivery via lipid nanoparticles containing 113-O12B ionizable lipid</h2><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0165">We synthesized two distinct LNP formulations: conventional cholesterol-based LNPs (CH_113) and DC-cholesterol-modified LNPs (DC_113), each comprising the 113-O12B ionizable lipid, DSPC, cholesterol or DC-cholesterol, and DSPE-PEG<sub>2k</sub> in the molar ratio 40:33.7:25:1.3 (Fig. 1A). The employed ionizable lipid 113-O12B has a chemical structure featuring three tertiary amine groups in its central region connected by ethylene linkers, four ester bond linkers, and four oleyl carbon chains with disulfide </div></section></section></section><section><section id="s0150"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">Conclusions</h2><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0460">In this study, we developed a lung-specific mRNA delivery system through the strategic substitution of conventional cholesterol with DC-cholesterol in LNP formulation containing 113-O12B ionizable lipid. The resulting LNPs demonstrated exceptional specificity toward lung tissue and lung tumors following systemic administration, with the targeting mechanism mediated through in situ endogenous protein interactions between DC_113 and Vtn. This novel approach leverages the natural protein corona</div></section></section><section><section id="s0155"><h2 class="section-title u-h4 u-margin-l-top u-margin-xs-bottom">CRediT authorship contribution statement</h2><div class="u-margin-s-bottom" id="p0470"><strong>Yoon Tae Goo:</strong> Writing – review &amp; editing, Writing – original draft, Validation, Methodology, Investigation, Data curation, Conceptualization. <strong>Vladislav Grigoriev:</strong> Investigation, Data curation. <strong>Tetiana Korzun:</strong> Investigation. <strong>Ammar Salem:</strong> Methodology. <strong>Kongbrailatpam Shitaljit Sharma:</strong> Methodology. <strong>Prem Singh:</strong> Investigation. <strong>Chrissa Kioussi:</strong> Data curation. <strong>Olena R. 		function safelyParseJson(key, value) {
	try {
		return JSON.parse(value);
	} catch {
		console.warn(`[parseValue(${key})] failed to parse ${value}`);
		return null;
	}
}
		function localStorageCache(localStorage, { propertyName: key, ttl }) {
	const freshValue = (cacheEntry) => {
		const expired = typeof cacheEntry.ttl === 'number' && cacheEntry.ttl < Date.now();
		if (expired) {
			localStorage.removeItem(key);
			return null;
		}

		return cacheEntry.value;
	};

	return {
		get() {
			const item = localStorage.getItem(key);
			if (!item) {
				return null;
			}

			const cacheEntry = safelyParseJson(key, item);
			if (!cacheEntry) {
				return null;
			}

			return freshValue(cacheEntry);
		},
		set(value) {
			const cacheEntry = { ttl: Date.now() + ttl, value };
			const item = JSON.stringify(cacheEntry);

			try {
				localStorage.setItem(key, item);
			} catch (e) {
				console.debug('[localStorageCache.set] error', e);
			}
		},
	};
}
		function withCache(cache) {
	return (supplier) => async () => {
		const cachedValue = cache.get();
		if (cachedValue) {
			return cachedValue;
		}

		const value = await supplier();
		if (value) {
			cache.set(value);
		}

		return value;
	};
}
		function setupWaitForEntitlements({ retryDelay, maxAttempts }) {
	return async () => {
		for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= maxAttempts; attempt++) {
			const rhfEntitlements = typeof window !== 'undefined' && window.rhfEntitlements;
			if (rhfEntitlements?.adobe) {
				return rhfEntitlements;
			}

			if (attempt < maxAttempts) {
				await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, retryDelay));
			}
		}
	};
}
		function setupGetEntitlements(options) {
	const localStorage = typeof window !== 'undefined' && window.localStorage;
	const withLocalStorageCache = localStorage
		? withCache(localStorageCache(localStorage, options))
		: undefined;

	const waitForEntitlements = setupWaitForEntitlements(options);
	return withLocalStorageCache ? withLocalStorageCache(waitForEntitlements) : waitForEntitlements;
}
		async function getAdobeDetails(options) {
	const { enabled, propertyName } = options;
	if (!enabled) {
		return {};
	}

	const getEntitlements = setupGetEntitlements(options);
	const entitlements = await getEntitlements();
	const details = entitlements?.adobe;
	if (!details) {
		console.warn(
			`[getAdobeDetails] did not find ${propertyName} in local storage or rhfEntitlements`,
		);
		return {};
	}

	return details;
}
	

	  async function tryGetAdobeDetails(options) {
	try {
		return await getAdobeDetails(options);
	} catch (error) {
		console.error(`Error in getAdobeDetails:`, error);
		return {};
	}
}
	  async function sendPageLoadEvent(options) {
	const loadTime =
		typeof performance !== 'undefined' ? Math.round(performance.now()).toString() : '';

	const { details, testId } = await tryGetAdobeDetails(options);
	if (details) {
		window.pageData.visitor.details = details;
	}
	if (testId) {
		window.pageData.page.testId = window.pageData.page.testId
			? `${window.pageData.page.testId},${testId}`
			: testId;
	}

	window.pageData.page.loadTime = loadTime;
	window.appData.push({
		event: 'pageLoad',
		...window.pageData,
	});
}

		function getTTLItem(key) {
	const itemStr = localStorage.getItem(key);
	let item;
	try {
		item = JSON.parse(itemStr);
	} catch {
		console.warn(`[getTTLItem(${key})] failed to parse ${itemStr}`);
		return null;
	}

	const expired = typeof item?.ttl !== 'number' || item?.ttl < Date.now();
	if (expired) {
		return null;
	}
	return item.value;
}
	  async function waitForLocalStorage({ retryDelay, maxAttempts, key }) {
	for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= maxAttempts; attempt++) {
		const rhfEntitlements = getTTLItem(key);
		if (rhfEntitlements) {
			return rhfEntitlements;
		}

		if (attempt < maxAttempts) {
			await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, retryDelay));
		}
	}
	console.warn(`[waitForLocalStorage(${key})] did not find item`);
	return null;
}

    pendo.addAltText = function () {
      var target = document.querySelector('body');
      var observer = new MutationObserver(function (mutations) {
        mutations.forEach(function (mutation) {
          if (mutation?.addedNodes?.length) {
            if (mutation.addedNodes[0]?.className?.includes("_pendo-badge")) {
              const badge = mutation.addedNodes[0];
              const altText = badge?.attributes['aria-label']?.value || 'Feedback';

        characterData: true,
        subtree: false
      };

      <script data-cfasync="false">
      (function initOneTrust()  {
        const monitor = {
  init: () => {},
  loaded: () => {},
};
        function enableGroup(group) {
	document.querySelectorAll(`script[type*="ot-${group}"]`).forEach((script) => {
		script.type = 'text/javascript';
		document.head.appendChild(script);
	});
}
        function runOneTrustCookies(doClear, monitor) {
	const oneTrustConsentSdkId = 'onetrust-consent-sdk';
	const emptyNodeSelectors = 'h3.ot-host-name, h4.ot-host-desc, button.ot-host-box';
	const ariaLabelledByButtonNodes = 'div.ot-accordion-layout > button';
	const ariaAttribute = 'aria-labelledby';

	function adjustOneTrustDOM() {
		const oneTrustRoot = document.getElementById('onetrust-consent-sdk');

		/* remove empty nodes */
		[...(oneTrustRoot?.querySelectorAll(emptyNodeSelectors) ?? [])]
			.filter((e) => e.textContent === '')
			.forEach((e) => {
				e.remove();
			});

		/* remove invalid aria-labelledby values */
		oneTrustRoot?.querySelectorAll(ariaLabelledByButtonNodes).forEach((e) => {
			const presentIdValue = e
				.getAttribute(ariaAttribute)
				?.split(' ')
				.filter((label) => document.getElementById(label))
				.join(' ');

			if (presentIdValue) {
				e.setAttribute(ariaAttribute, presentIdValue);
			}
		});
	}

	function observeOneTrustLoaded(shouldSetOTDefaults, isConsentPresent) {
		const cb = (mutationList, observer) => {
			const oneTrustRoot = mutationList
				.filter(
					(mutationRecord) =>
						mutationRecord.type === 'childList' && mutationRecord.addedNodes.length,
				)
				.flatMap((mutationRecord) => [...mutationRecord.addedNodes])
				.find((e) => e.id === oneTrustConsentSdkId);

			if (oneTrustRoot && typeof OneTrust !== 'undefined') {
				monitor.loaded(true);
				OneTrust.OnConsentChanged(() => {
					const perfAllowed =
						decodeURIComponent(document.cookie.match('(^| )OptanonConsent=([^;]+)')?.[2])
							?.match('groups=([0-9:0|1,?]+)&?')?.[1]
							?.match('2:([0|1])')[1] === '1';

					if (perfAllowed) {
						enableGroup('performance');
					}
				});

				if (
					!isConsentPresent &&
					(shouldSetOTDefaults || OneTrust.GetDomainData().ConsentModel.Name === 'implied consent')
				) {
					OneTrust.UpdateConsent(`Category`, `1:1`);
					OneTrust.UpdateConsent(`Category`, `2:1`);
					OneTrust.UpdateConsent(`Category`, `3:1`);
					OneTrust.UpdateConsent(`Category`, `8:0`);
				}

				document.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('@sdtech/onetrust/loaded', {}));

				observer.disconnect();
				adjustOneTrustDOM();
			}
		};

		const observer = new MutationObserver(cb);
		observer.observe(document.querySelector('body'), { childList: true });
	}

	if (doClear) {
		document.cookie =
			'OptanonAlertBoxClosed=; expires=Thu, 01 Jan 1970 00:00:00 UTC; samesite=lax; path=/';
	}

	const isConsentPresent = !!decodeURIComponent(
		document.cookie.match('(^| )OptanonConsent=([^;]+)')?.[2],
	)?.match('groups=([0-9:0|1,?]+)&?')?.[1];
	const shouldSetOTDefaults =
		'true' === 'false' && !document.cookie?.match('OptanonAlertBoxClosed=');
	if (shouldSetOTDefaults) {
		const date = new Date();
		date.setFullYear(date.getFullYear() + 1);
		document.cookie = `OptanonAlertBoxClosed=${new Date().toISOString()}; expires=${date.toUTCString()}; samesite=lax; path=/; domain=sciencedirect.com`;
	}

	observeOneTrustLoaded(shouldSetOTDefaults, isConsentPresent, monitor);

	window.addOTScript = () => {
		const otSDK = document.createElement('script');
		otSDK.setAttribute('data-cfasync', 'false');
		otSDK.setAttribute('src', 'https://cdn.cookielaw.org/scripttemplates/otSDKStub.js');
		otSDK.setAttribute('data-document-language', 'true');
		otSDK.setAttribute('data-domain-script', '865ea198-88cc-4e41-8952-1df75d554d02');
		window.addOTScript = () => {};
		document.head.appendChild(otSDK);
		monitor.init();
	};
	window.addEventListener('load', () => window.addOTScript());
}
        if (document.location.host.match(/\.sciencedirect\.com$/)) {
          runOneTrustCookies(true, monitor);
        }
        else {
          window.addEventListener('load', (event) => {
            enableGroup('performance');
          });
        }
      }());
    </script>
